2 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Guide to El Niño: What It Is, Why It Matters, and What to Do About It
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
As crude oil flirts with April’s highs, and the price of diesel sits comfortably above $6 per
gallon, disruption to the global energy complex has emerged as a major theme of 2026.
Add yet another variable to the equation: the current “El Niño” event, on track to be one

of the strongest on record. Below we answer frequently asked questions on El Niño,
the potential impact on the global economy and markets and, most importantly, how
investors can position portfolios accordingly.
What is El Niño, and what makes this time different? El Niño is the warm phase
of the “El Niño-Southern Oscillation,” a natural climate pattern occurring every two to
seven years. Simply put, El Niño refers to the warming of sea surface temperatures in

the eastern Pacific, historically leading to drier conditions in some areas (Southeast Asia,
Australia, Central America, southern Africa) and wetter conditions in others (southern
U.S., parts of Latin America) (Exhibit 1).
What makes this episode different? Should forecasts prove correct, this El Niño is on
track to be historic. The National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration’s latest
outlook assigns at least an 83% probability that El Niño’s strength during October-

December will exceed that of any previous episode since 1950. Already, its impact has
been felt via wildfires in Indonesia, the weakest monsoon season in India since 2015, 1
and severe flooding from Paraguay to Peru. The upshot: more extreme weather, hotter
temperatures globally, lower crop production in commodity-exporting economies, and
higher prices across major agricultural products.
Exhibit 1: El Niño's Impact Varies Globally.
Geography Typical rainfall pattern Specific impacts

South Asia Weaker summer monsoon risk Lower crop yields, water shortages, food inflation
Southeast Asia Drier Lower rice and palm oil production, wildfire risk, food inflation
United States Wetter in the south; warmer/drier in the north Southern flood risk; lower northern heating demand
Central America & Northern South America Drier Drought, lower crop yields, water shortages, food inflation, lower hydropower output
Southern Africa Drier Weaker maize harvests, food inflation, lower hydropower output

Western South America Wetter along the Pacific coast Flooding, infrastructure damage, disrupted fisheries
Southern South America Wetter Flooding and crop damage; potential yield gains in some areas
Australia Drier Drought, agricultural losses, elevated wildfire risk, food inflation
Source: National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration, World Meteorological Organization, Food and Agriculture Organization, Australian Bureau of Meteorology, World Bank. Data as of

October 30, 2026.
How will El Niño impact the global economy and markets? Which regions will be
most affected? El Niño’s impact on the global economy and markets is likely to be felt
most via commodity price inflation. Alongside higher energy and fertilizer costs resulting
from war in Europe and the Middle East, El Niño could put further upward pressure on
commodities like wheat, sugar, corn, soy, cocoa and coffee as drought (or extreme rain)
weighs on crop yields and production. Latest estimates from the United Nations Food

and Agriculture Organization (FAO) suggest global food prices were up 5.8% year-over-
year (YoY) in September (Exhibit 2). Livestock is also likely to be challenged by higher
temperatures globally, while flooding has already threatened copper production in
Chile. Meanwhile, with hydropower accounting for more than half of the average annual
electricity generation in Brazil, Colombia, Peru and other Latin American countries, 2
below-average rainfall in affected areas has huge implications for electricity generation.

As an example, Colombia’s natural gas demand nearly doubled during the 2023-2024 El
Niño event.2 Another key watchpoint will be whether drier conditions reduce water levels
1 India Meteorological Department, Bloomberg. Refers to rainfall between June and September as of September 30, 2026.
2 International Energy Agency, as of October 7, 2026
Investment Implications
We expect El Niño to place upward
pressure on agricultural goods
prices globally. We continue to

see commodities as a strategic
diversifier and hedge against inflation
in portfolios. The U.S. remains our
preferred region with companies in
Industrials and Materials as potential
beneficiaries. Meanwhile, economic
impacts to the emerging world will vary
by country but the broader Emerging
Market (EM) equity market could be
less exposed given its significant tilt
toward Technology.

### Page 3

3 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
in the Panama Canal and other key shipping routes, particularly in light of the Strait of
Hormuz closure.
These pressures won’t be equally distributed and will heavily depend on this El Niño’s
severity. Among the more vulnerable to higher agricultural prices will be heavily import-
dependent countries in the Middle East, Africa, and parts of Asia. So too will economies
that count food as a larger share of consumer spending such as the Philippines (36%),

Indonesia (33%), Egypt (33%), India (28%), and Vietnam (26%) (Exhibit 3). Developed
economies like the U.S. (6%), Canada (10%), and Germany (11%) stand to be better
insulated from a food-related shock to headline inflation.
Within the U.S., some impacts could be positive with a potentially milder winter reducing
heating costs and wetter conditions in the South improving agricultural conditions. A recent
paper from the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco found that, in addition to putting

upward pressure on personal consumption expenditures (PCE) inflation, strong El Niño
episodes have been historically associated with a temporary boost to employment in the
following year via better construction conditions in the North and weather-event recovery
efforts.3 In California and parts of the Southwest, El Niño will be yet another stress test for
property insurers as flooding and landslide risks increase.
For more vulnerable developing economies, meanwhile, higher food and energy costs could

present a policy dilemma. On one hand, governments in vulnerable areas may move to
introduce fiscal relief to support households and boost growth. History points to plenty of
examples of this, such as Indonesia’s cash assistance in late 2023 or Thailand’s $1.8 billion
in loans to farmers in 2015. On the other hand, elevated inflation expectations and, in
some cases, weaker currencies could limit EM central banks’ room to ease monetary policy,
particularly with several major developed central banks aligned in hiking interest rates. Also

complicating matters: Global sovereign debt surpassed $110 trillion in Q2 2026, leaving
less fiscal space to absorb incoming shocks. 4
How can investors position portfolios accordingly? In many ways, the prospect of a
very strong El Niño only reinforces structural trends we’ve identified as defining the coming
years: a return to higher inflation and higher interest rates; elevated commodity prices
amid heightened geopolitical risk and resource/supply shortages; and greater investment in

energy infrastructure and supply chain resilience.
Considering these themes, we continue to like commodities not just as a hedge against
higher inflation but also as a diversification tool in portfolios given their negative correlation
with Equities and bonds. That’s not to mention secular forces placing upward pressure on
commodity prices such as the resource-intensive AI buildout and broader protectionism. To
wit, the Bloomberg Commodity Index, which spans oil, gold, various metals and agricultural

goods has gained nearly 30% this year, handily outperforming the S&P 500 (+1%) and the
Bloomberg U.S. Aggregate Bond Index (-1%).
Within Equities, the U.S. is still our preferred region. While upside risk to inflation remains
a risk to watch going into 2027, food is a relatively small share of the consumer basket in
the U.S., and neither strong services spending nor capital investment should be materially
affected by El Niño. Companies within the Industrials and Materials sectors focused on

precision agriculture, water management and crop protection may be positioned to benefit.
Import-dependent EM economies remain particularly exposed, though impacts will vary
widely depending on the magnitude and timing of weather events. It’s worth noting too that
EM Equities remain heavily concentrated in East Asian markets with substantial exposure to
technology, leaving the broader index arguably less exposed to the economic ramifications
of El Niño than in past episodes. Indeed, even after recent weakness in semiconductor

names, Taiwan, Korea, and China together account for a near-70% of the broader MSCI
Emerging Markets Index. 5 We maintain a slight overweight to the region in portfolios given
earnings strength but favor an active approach.

MARKET VIEW
Another Tale of Market Rotation
Kirsten Cabacungan, Vice President and Senior Investment Strategist
October offered another reminder that headline index returns do not always tell the
full story. The S&P 500 ended the month slightly lower, down almost 1%, but the more
muted headline return masked meaningful shifts in market leadership beneath the
surface. At the center of the volatility was a sharp unwind in the AI trade, particularly

across semiconductors and memory stocks, with the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index
experiencing a 19% intramonth drawdown. The dominant concerns in recent months
have centered on the sustainability of AI infrastructure spending and whether the scale
of investment will ultimately generate sufficient returns. Those only intensified amid a
sharp rise in bond yields after the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield spiked above 5.3% as of
October 5 for the first time since 2002, raising scrutiny over AI financing conditions and

whether higher borrowing costs could increase hurdle rates for continued investment
and slow the pace of capital spending.
Yet the pressure on AI market leaders has not translated into a broad retreat from U.S.
Equities. Instead, capital has moved toward previously lagging areas of the market,
with Value-oriented, defensive and rate-sensitive stocks gaining ground. Value defeated
Growth in October, with the Russell 1000 Value Index outperforming its Growth

counterpart by 10 percentage points, an outcome seen in only about 1% of months since
1979. The move extends Value's outperformance for the year, with the index up 23%
year-to-date compared with down almost 1% for Growth. Participation also improved.
The equal-weighted S&P 500 outperformed its market-cap-weighted counterpart by its
widest monthly gap since 2009 (Exhibit 4A), while the share of S&P 500 constituents
trading above their 200-day moving average rebounded to 62% from just 43% at the

end of September, and 76% of stocks outperformed the index for the month.

The latest rotation is not the first attempt at a change in market leadership this year.
The more important question is what that shift represents: a fundamental reassessment
of the AI trade or another step toward broader, more mature market leadership?
Recent Q3 Technology earnings results have sharpened that debate. Investors are
increasingly looking beyond the scale of AI spending to companies’ ability to translate
that investment into durable growth and attractive returns. The result may be greater

dispersion not only between Growth and Value, but also among AI leaders as investors
become more discerning about which companies can justify the scale of their
investments.
Investment Implications
We continue to look for potential
missing opportunities and favor
diversification across and within asset
classes in a multi-asset portfolio.
In particular, we see potential
opportunities to broaden exposure
beyond AI leaders toward areas
where earnings, capital spending and

productivity trends are improving,
including Value, Small- and Mid-caps,
and select non-U.S. markets, particularly
EMs and Japan. As market leadership
evolves, maintaining diversified
exposures and periodically rebalancing
portfolios can help investors participate
in a broader set of possible return
drivers without becoming overly
dependent on any single theme.

### Page 5

5 of 8 November 2, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
But greater selectivity does not necessarily signal the end of the AI investment cycle.
YoY growth rates may become harder to maintain after two years of rapid acceleration
as the comparison base rises, but absolute levels of AI capital spending remain elevated.
What matters more is whether spending translates into strong productivity and earnings
growth ahead and whether AI demand continues to support investment. October’s

volatility may therefore reflect less a retreat from the AI theme and more a recalibration
of valuations and expected returns, as other areas of the market begin to contribute
more meaningfully to market performance.
Several developments ahead will help determine whether that rotation has staying
power. One important test is whether broader participation can persist if pressure on AI
stocks continues. Continued strength outside the AI trade would support the case that

investors are reallocating capital rather than broadly reducing risk. The path of monetary
policy will also matter as investors assess where borrowing costs may be headed. The
Federal Reserve’s not-so-hawkish hold in October offered some reassurance, but the
path ahead remains dependent on the evolving balance between inflation and growth.
Any signs of resolution of geopolitical tensions remain an important swing factor
particularly as recent flashpoints in the Middle East have pressured energy prices higher

again. Results of the midterm elections should also provide greater visibility into the
policy landscape for the next few years. While the market does not need every clearing
event to resolve favorably, greater clarity around these uncertainties could help narrow
the range of potential outcomes facing investors.
For investors, October’s sudden change in leadership reinforces the importance of
diversification. Leadership can shift quickly, leaving portfolios that become overly

dependent on a single theme, sector or style particularly vulnerable when it does. We
remain constructive on U.S. Equities and believe that the market may be transitioning
more toward a mid-cycle phase in which returns become broader but also more
measured compared to the sharp acceleration earlier in the cycle. That argues not for
abandoning the market’s long-term winners, but for complementing them with exposure
to additional sources of returns across style, size, sector and region.

THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
Reorienting around AI: From Corporate Plans to Construction to Capital Goods
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
For all the questions (and recent market weakness) hanging over the AI trade, from
monetization and cash burn to financing risk and overcapacity, a measurable aspect is
the buildout itself. Data center construction is lifting construction spending and capital

goods orders, while companies are adding and reviving capacity to meet AI-related
demand. Still, planning delays, access to power, labor scarcity, local politics and the cost
of capital remain meaningful constraints on the buildout’s pace or profitability.
Corporate Plans. Take Caterpillar as one example that has resumed production of a
10-megawatt natural gas turbine discontinued back in 2022. A 175-year old glassmaker,
Corning, is leaning into optical connectivity as AI clusters require faster connections of

optical fiber cabling between servers. Caterpillar and Corning are just two companies
among others pivoting to supply data centers.
Construction to Capital Goods. The AI reorientation is evident in construction
spending as well, where data center construction has outpaced every other major
residential and nonresidential category. Over the last year AI-related construction on data
centers has surged more than 60% while spending on residential, lodging, commercial

or office has declined notably (Exhibit 5A). Data center construction is running at an $85
billion (B) annualized rate this year, versus roughly $49B a year earlier, and now accounts
for 11% of private nonresidential construction, up from just 2% in 2022 according to
the Census Bureau. Similarly, demand for artificial intelligence business equipment and
transportation equipment drove an increase in durable goods orders in September on the
back of new orders for U.S.-manufactured capital goods, suggesting business spending

on equipment remained robust in Q3. Another factor, see Exhibit 5B, where critical
components such as generators, switchboards, chillers and transformers continue to
face long lead times that affect delivery timelines and ultimate profitability for new data
center development.
These signposts reinforce our view that we are only in the middle innings of the AI
investment cycle. From an investment perspective, huge demand for data centers, power
systems, networking infrastructure, specialized chips and other equipment continues

to strain supply, creating both bottlenecks and potential opportunities for companies
powering the buildout.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500/Market-cap Weighted Index is a market-capitalization-weighted index that is widely regarded as the best single gauge of large-cap U.S. equities. The index includes 500 leading

companies and covers approximately 80% of available market capitalization.
S&P 500 Equal-Weighted Index includes the same constituents as the capitalization weighted S&P 500, but each company in the S&P 500 EWI is allocated a fixed weight - or 0.2% of the
index total at each quarterly rebalance.
Bloomberg U.S. Aggregate Bond Index is a broad-based, market-capitalization-weighted benchmark that measures the performance of the U.S. investment-grade, U.S. dollar-denominated,

fixed-rate taxable bond market
Bloomberg Commodity Index is a broadly diversified financial benchmark that tracks the price movements of physical commodities through futures contracts.
MSCI Emerging Market Index captures large and mid cap representation across 24 Emerging Markets (EM) countries.
United Nations Food and Agriculture Food Price Index is a measure of the monthly change in international prices of a basket of food commodities.
Philadelphia Semiconductor Index is a market-cap-weighted index focusing on 30 leading semiconductor firms, offering insights into the tech market.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.
Nonfinancial assets, such as closely held businesses, real estate, fine art, oil, gas and mineral properties, and timber, farm and ranch land, are complex in nature and involve risks including total

## Document 313: Cliff Notes_ managing expectations (2026-11-08)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ managing expectations (2026-11-08).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-08
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: managing expectations
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
11:04 October 23 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
With little domestic data of significance, participants’ attention focused on RBA Deputy Governor
Hauser's fireside chat, which saw an abrupt shift in market expectations of a follow-up rate hike at the
November meeting.
Hauser warned in his remarks that the current inflation outlook was "materially worse" than the RBA's

### Page 2

August forecasts. Three upside inflation risks were characterised as having "crystallised" ahead of the
September meeting – the Middle East, the AI boom, and domestic supply constraints. Hauser noted the
Middle East picture "looks worse" given oil prices are "about a third higher than [the RBA] assumed in
August”. He then positioned AI and data centre developments among an "extraordinary sequence of
positive demand shocks around the world", with "higher AI prices, tech prices feeding through into

CPI". Finally, on domestic capacity pressures, the Deputy Governor noted that "inflation's been coming
in at or a little bit above expectations, despite an apparent easing in the labour market". He went on to
state that the staff are currently assessing "whether [the RBA] need to change [its] judgement on
capacity pressures in the labour market again" – in other words, revise up their assumption for the
NAIRU.
Hauser's comments prompted a material reassessment of market pricing for a follow-up November

hike, from just a 20% chance at the end of last week to a 50/50 proposition today ahead of next week's
Q3 CPI. For that release, we are forecasting a 1.3% (4.1%yr) lift in headline inflation and a 0.9 (3.5%yr)
increase in trimmed mean inflation. Our preview provides more detail behind the component mix for Q3
as well as our upward revisions to the preliminary Q4 forecast. Provided inflation prints broadly in line
with our expectations, the RBA is likely to hike by 25bp at the November meeting.

Across in New Zealand, consumer prices rose 0.7% in Q3 and annual inflation eased from 4.1%yr to
3.7%yr. Under the surface of this ‘as expected’ read though, several closely watched measures of core
inflation took a step higher to now be at or slightly above the top of the RBNZ’s target band. Our NZ
economics team consequently expect the RBNZ will raise the OCR 25bps to 3.00% at its October
policy review, while retaining a 4.00% terminal OCR forecast for September 2027.

Earlier in the week, China's economy surprised to the upside in Q3, GDP growth accelerating from
0.9%qtr to 1.2%qtr, lifting annual growth from 4.3%yr to 4.5%yr – keeping authorities on track to
achieve their 4.5-5.0% full-year growth objective.
While annual retail sales growth improved in September, rising from 0.4%yr to 1.1%yr, the current pace
of growth is only a quarter of the gain seen just a year ago and very weak versus history. Consumer
spending continues to be constrained by weak confidence, particularly with respect to employment

prospects and wealth. On housing wealth, five years into the property downturn, house prices continue
to decline month-on-month and investment remains in a deep downturn, -20.6%ytd.
Total fixed asset investment has been detracting from annual GDP growth since April owing to a lull in
high-tech investment, a lack of confidence in future demand amongst domestically oriented businesses
as well as property’s malaise. For aggregate growth to hold up through 2027 and 2028 as the

contribution from net exports moderates, broad-based stimulus in scale will prove necessary. Expect
recent targeted support measures to be broadened and given much greater scale in coming months.
Finally, in the UK, inflation accelerated from 3.1%yr to 3.5%yr in September, exceeding both market
expectations and the Bank of England's July forecast. The surprise was largely driven by energy, fuel
prices rising 5.4%mth. However, core inflation also edged up from 2.6%yr to 2.8%yr as services

inflation increased from 3.4%yr to 3.7%yr. While not an immediate concern inflation, the labour market
showing resilience, the ILO unemployment rate falling from 4.9% to 4.7% in August, suggests demand

### Page 3

Related articles
risks for inflation will need close monitoring as well as supply-side concerns.
Next week will see the focus once again return to the US as the FOMC meet and US Q3 GDP is
released. For both events, the implications for risks to the outlook will be the focus. The week after, the
US mid-term elections are due.

## Document 358: GLOBAL MARKETS CALL_ Government Bond Yields Ease, Gold Falls

Source PDF: all report pdf/GLOBAL MARKETS CALL_ Government Bond Yields Ease, Gold Falls.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-01
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: GLOBAL MARKETS CALL: Government Bond Yields Ease, Gold Falls
Date:    43 minutes ago
GLOBAL MARKETS CALL: Government Bond Yields Ease, Gold Falls

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
GLOBAL MARKETS CALL:
Government Bond Yields Ease,
Gold Falls
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 1 Nov 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Gaël Gaborel - OrbisTerrae / Unsplash
Investors sought safety in government bonds last week as the AI selloff
rattled stock markets worldwide. Yields eased across most of the major
bond markets. The stock market selloff has been hardest on the markets
that soared the most this year. October is ending with the US well ahead
of the rest of the world. That validates our recent shift to market weight on
the Go Global portfolio strategy. Here’s more:

I. Stock Market Performance
The Stay Home vs Go Global ratios have rebounded sharply since mid-
year, driven by US outperformance (chart). They remain below their early
2025 peaks but are heading back up.
The US MSCI’s share of the All Country World MSCI’s market cap has
risen back to 65.3% (chart). Its share of forward earnings is 54.3%. That
11-point gap is the widest since January 2025. US stocks again are
pricing in far more than their share of global earnings.

### Page 3

October’s biggest losers were this year’s AI winners. South Korea finished
the month at the bottom of the country ETF rankings, down 16.4% in
dollars (chart). Taiwan fell 10.4%. Switzerland topped the board with an
8.8% gain, followed by Mexico (4.8%). The US slipped 0.9%, while the All
Country World ex-US ETF fell 2.9%.
II. Stock Markets Earnings & Valuation

### Page 4

Overseas forward earnings momentum is the strongest in over 15 years.
Forward earnings for the All Country World ex-US MSCI is up 41.7% y/y,
topping its 2021 post-pandemic peak (chart). Forward revenues is up
11.6% y/y. Earnings outpacing revenues by that much implies forward
profit margins are widening significantly.
Japan leads the major markets in forward earnings growth since early
2009 (chart). Its forward earnings has risen 720.7% in yen. The US is up

524.6%. EM forward earnings has surged this year and is now up 224.7%
over the same period. The EMU and the UK have barely doubled.
Valuation multiples are falling even as earnings soar (chart). The US
MSCI’s forward P/E has dropped to 19.1 from 23.7 a year ago. Japan’s is

### Page 5

14.7, and the EMU’s is 13.9. The UK trades at 11.9. EM trades at 8.9, its
lowest since the 2008 financial crisis.
III. Fixed Income
Government bond yields eased last week in most major markets in
response to the AI selloff (chart). The US 10-year Treasury yield has fallen
to 4.91% from 5.00% a week earlier. France’s 10-year government bond
yield has eased to 4.98%. The UK’s 5.30% and Australia’s 5.14% remain
the highest in the group. Japan’s yield has dropped to 2.76%.

IV. Gold
We've been bullish on the gold price since it jumped to a new record high
above $2,000 per ounce in early 2024. We reckoned that buying by the

### Page 6

central banks of countries that feared the US and Europe might freeze
their forex reserves, as happened to Russia after it invaded Ukraine,
which would be bullish for gold. That was true until the war started in the
Middle East on February 28, 2026 (chart).
We aren't sure why gold hasn't bounced off of its key support levels.
Instead, it dropped back below $4,000 last week. The next support is
around $3,400. Rising interest rates may explain why gold has been so

weak if central bank buying is being swamped by selling from private
investors.
The People's Bank of China is still buying gold (chart). Chinese investors
have lost lots of money in their property and stock markets. Now, they
may be bailing out of gold.

### Page 7

Is there still a case for gold to reach a price of $10,000 per ounce by the
end of the decade? Yes, but the case is getting weaker. If the S&P 500
reaches 10,000 by the end of the decade, investors might rebalance their
portfolios by allocating more to gold (chart).
V. Focus On China & Brazil
China’s MSCI stock price index has fallen to 66.4 in dollars, down from
92.0 at its 2025 high (chart). The property bust and deflation continue to
weigh on the market. So does the rapidly aging population.

Chinese tech has missed the AI boom entirely (chart). The China
Technology ETF (CQQQ) has fallen to 37.78, 65.2% below its February
2021 record high. The Nasdaq-100 ETF (QQQ) is 9.5% below its record
high. We continue to favor EM ex-China over China.

### Page 8

In Brazil, President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva won the October 25 runoff
with nearly 52% of valid votes after trailing Senator Flávio Bolsonaro in the
first round. Investors had positioned for a Bolsonaro win, and Bolsonaro
says he will challenge the result in court. The Brazil MSCI stock price
index fell to 3,508.86 in local currency, giving back most of its post-first-
round gains (chart). It is now up just 1.0% ytd in local currency and 2.2%
in dollars.

log in to the Yardeni QuickTakes website and post them at the end
of the QuickTakes article. Paid members' contributions may be
featured in our segment, "Ed Answers Your Questions".

## Document 365: Intel出事它卻沒事？營收上升但毛利下降？ 財報解剖_ AMD_ 26年11月

Source PDF: all report pdf/Intel出事它卻沒事？營收上升但毛利下降？ 財報解剖_ AMD_ 26年11月.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-04
Pages: 23
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    KP - FOMO 研究院  <fomosoc@substack.com>
Subject: Intel 出事它卻沒事？營收上升但⽑利下降？  財報解剖 : AMD| 26 年 11 ⽉
Date:    3 hours ago
Intel 出事它卻沒事？營收上升但⽑利下降？  財報解剖 : AMD| 26 年 11 ⽉
View in browser
Intel 出事它卻沒事？營收上升但⽑利
下降？  財報解剖 : AMD| 26 年 11 ⽉
KP@FOMOSOC AND BENITA HSU
NOV 4 ∙ PAID
READ IN APP
10 ⽉ 6 ⽇到 11 ⽉ 3 ⽇財報公布前， AMD 的股價從 649.42 美元⼀路跌到 483.90 美
元，不到⼀個⽉摔掉了 165.52 美元（跌幅 25.5% ）。
光是 10 ⽉ 30 ⽇ Intel 發出 PC 賣不動的警訊那天， AMD 單⽇就被拖累⼤跌近
11% 。
財報都還沒出來，市場已經先把最壞的劇本演了⼀遍。

### Page 2

結果 11 ⽉ 3 ⽇盤後財報⼀開牌， AMD 盤後股價⽴刻反彈。
字⾯上的成績單確實漂亮：
第三季總營收年增 45% 來到 134 億美元，第四季營收指引更直接喊到 170 億美
元（年增 66% ），其中「資料中⼼」部⾨第三季營收年增 97% ，幾乎翻倍。

### Page 3

執⾏⻑蘇姿丰更在法說會上預告， 2027 年全公司總營收將再成⻑超過 80% 。
但翻開財報細項，卻會看到兩個耐⼈尋味的反差：
明明營收與獲利都在創紀錄，第三季真正流進⼝袋的「⾃由現⾦流」卻⼤
減了 64% ；⽽且為了綁定⼤客戶所送出的股票（認股權證），還在帳上砸出
了近 11 億美元的業外損失。
這份財報真正要回答的問題是：
財報前跌掉的那 25% ，有多少是市場錯殺？
當 AMD 準備邁向近千億美元的營收規模，這⾨⼤⽣意背後的真實利潤與現⾦
流究竟⻑什麼樣⼦？
接下來，我們就⽤最⽩話的⽅式，把這場轉變背後的財務邏輯⼀步步算清楚。

### Page 4

重點⼀｜ Helios 系統正式出貨：為什
麼資料中⼼賣越多，⽑利率反⽽微降？
1.1 什麼是 Helios ？只賣「核⼼晶⽚」與「設計圖」的⽣意模式
過去買晶⽚，客戶買回去⾃⼰插上伺服器就能⽤。
但現在的 AI 運算太龐⼤，客戶需要的是⼀整座已經幫你把上百顆晶⽚、網路線
與散熱系統全部串聯好的「⼤型機櫃」。
AMD 推出的 Helios ，就是這樣⼀套「整座機櫃的設計藍圖」。
特別的是， AMD ⾃⼰只負責賣機櫃裡最昂貴的核⼼⼤腦（包含 Instinct AI 晶
⽚、 EPYC 處理器、網路晶⽚與軟體），⾄於外⾯的⾦屬機殼、電源供應器與整
機組裝，則交給像 HPE 、 Dell 、 Super Micro 這類品牌伺服器⼤廠與系統夥伴去
出貨並向客戶收費。
這代表 AMD 不必賺利潤微薄的鐵櫃與組裝費，帳⾯認列的營收主要來⾃⾼單價
的核⼼晶⽚⽅案。
不過要注意的是，每⼀顆 AI 晶⽚裡都封裝了向記憶體⼤廠採購的⾼昂 HBM
（⾼頻寬記憶體），這筆硬體採購成本會直接算進 AMD 的銷貨成本裡；再加上

為了搶⼤單所給出的價格優惠，都會影響最終的⽑利率表現。
蘇姿丰在法說會上證實，新⼀代 MI455X 晶⽚已在第三季⼤量出貨，包括
Anthropic 、 Meta 、 Microsoft 、 OpenAI 與 Oracle 都已經讓第⼀批初期的 Helios 系
統上線運作並跑起模型。
到了第四季， AI 晶⽚的營收更將在歷史上⾸度超越傳統伺服器 CPU 。
這也帶動資料中⼼部⾨展現強⼤的賺錢效率：
第三季資料中⼼部⾨營業利益年增 168% 來到 29 億美元，相當於每做 100 美元⽣
意，就有約 34 美元是營業利益（較去年同期的 25 美元⼤幅提升）。
1.2 算⼀筆明細帳：新增資料中⼼營收的五成⽑利秘密
既然核⼼晶⽚這麼熱銷，為什麼 AMD 預估第四季全公司的⽑利率會從 56% 微
幅降到 55% ？

### Page 5

道理很像⼀家便當店接到了⼤型企業的團膳⼤單：
雖然為了搶下⼤單，每個便當的單價壓低了⼀點、⾷材成本也⾼，讓平均⽑利
率微幅下降，但因為⼀次賣出好幾千個，收銀機進帳的總獲利反⽽迎來⼤爆
發。
我們可以把第四季的財測數字拆開來，算算看 AMD 多賣的這批資料中⼼訂單
到底賺多少⽑利：
先算資料中⼼多賣了多少錢：
第四季總營收預計為  170 億美元，⽐第三季多出約  36 億美元。
但別忘了，第三季貢獻  37 億美元的「 PC 與電競部⾨」，第四季預計會衰退超
過  20% （⾄少少掉  7.4 億美元）。
⼀邊少賺、⼀邊還要讓總營收⼤增，算下來資料中⼼第四季營收必須單季暴增
約  43 億美元，相當於⼀個季度就⻑⼤了  50% 。

### Page 6

再算這批新增訂單的⽑利率：
第四季預估總⽑利約  93.5 億美元（ 170 億  × 55% ），⽐第三季多出  18.7 億美
元。
假設少掉的那  7.4 億美元  PC 業務⽑利率⼤約是  50% （此為推算假設），回推那
43 億美元新增的資料中⼼營收，每多賣  100 美元⼤約只能賺到  50 美元的⽑利
（邊際⽑利率粗估約  50% ）。
NVIDIA 整體的平均⽑利率⻑期⾼達 70% 以上，⽽ AMD 第四季度新增資料
中⼼營收（包含 AI 晶⽚與伺服器 CPU ）的邊際⽑利率粗估約落在 50% 左
右。
這組數字除了反映⾼昂的 HBM 記憶體成本外，也把「 AMD ⽤價格折扣與送
股票換取市占率」變成了⼀個具體可追蹤的財務指標。
當然，因為單季多賣了 43 億美元，規模放⼤的威⼒依然驚⼈，第四季全公司的
營業利益預計將從第三季的 39 億美元⼀路跳升⾄約 56 億美元。
重點⼆｜管理層喊出 2027 成⻑破
80% ：要達標，你得相信什麼？
2.1 短短 90 天內，再度⼤幅調⾼明年⽬標

⽐起第三季成績單，這場法說會真正的重頭戲，是管理層⼤幅調⾼了 2027 年的
成⻑⽬標。
蘇姿丰表⽰，過去 90 天內多家雲端巨頭擴⼤了採購計畫。
對⽐ 8 ⽉份法說會時管理層預估「明年伺服器 CPU 成⻑超過 70% 」，這次直接上
修為「明年伺服器 CPU 營收將接近翻倍」，資料中⼼ AI 晶⽚營收更將「成⻑遠
超過兩倍（ grow significantly more than twofold ）」，帶動資料中⼼佔 2027 年全公
司總營收達到 80% 以上。

### Page 7

更重要的是，管理層指出 2027 年伺服器 CPU 營收翻倍的動能來⾃三⼤塊：
傳統雲端與企業端汰換、 AI 代理（ Agentic AI ）⼯作負載，以及 AI 叢集的主節
點（ Head Nodes ）。
隨著新⼀代 EPYC Venice 處理器在第三季開始出貨（合作夥伴承諾開發的平台
數超過 240 個，⽐上⼀代多出兩成），這波 CPU 成⻑主要是由「出貨量增加」與
「⾼核⼼數產品組合」所驅動，⽽⾮單純靠漲價，這意味著企業與雲端升級
CPU 的需求⽐市場想像得更加紮實。
2.2 邁向 940 億美元的算術題：真正的上限在⼯廠產能
「 2027 年總營收成⻑超過 80% 」換算成⾦額有多⼤？
2026 年全年營收基準： 2026 年前三季營收加上第四季預估的  170 億美
元，今年全年總營收約為  521 億美元。

### Page 8

2027 年營收⽬標： 明年再成⻑超過  80% ，代表  2027 全年營收將⼀舉突破
939 億美元。這個數字已經追平了外資  Bernstein 先前最樂觀的預估（ 938
億美元），更超越了  Morgan Stanley 的預期（ 889 億美元）。
但要做到近 940 億美元並不容易。
就算把第四季創紀錄的 170 億美元直接乘以 4 個季度，⼀年也才 680 億美元，中
間還有 260 億美元的缺⼝。
這代表在 2027 年裡， AMD 每⼀季營收平均都得⽐上⼀季再⻑⼤ 13% 到 14% ，且
到了 2027 年第四季，單季營收必須衝上約 280 億美元。
蘇姿丰在問答環節坦⾔⽬前「訂單需求⼤於供給」。這說明 2027 年營收能不
能衝上 940 億美元，關鍵在於 AMD 能從供應商⼿上搶到多少⾼階記憶體與
晶⽚封裝產能。
重點三｜買晶⽚送股票：拆解「認股權
證」的代價與財報怪象
3.1 算算看送股票的代價： Meta 的領股條件⽐ OpenAI 更寬鬆
為了吸引雲端巨頭採⽤⾃家產品， AMD 先前與 OpenAI 及 Meta 簽署協議，分別

給予最⾼ 1.6 億股（合計 3.2 億股）、每股只需要花 0.01 美元就能認購的「附條
件認股權證」。
為什麼要送這麼⼤禮？
因為⼤客戶從 NVIDIA 換成 AMD ，得派⼤批⼯程師重寫軟體、重新設計機房，
轉換成本極⾼。
但仔細對⽐兩家巨頭的合約細節，會發現條件⼤不相同：
OpenAI （綁定採購量＋股價⾨檻）：
OpenAI 的  1.6 億股權證採分批解鎖，除了要買夠晶⽚，最後⼀批還規定要等
AMD 股價漲到  600 美元才能領⾛。
如果  OpenAI 最終全數領滿，這批股票的市值⾄少⾼達  960 億美元（ 1.6 億股  ×
600 美元）。
Meta （只綁採購與出貨⾥程碑，無股價⾨檻）：

### Page 9

Meta 的條件更為寬鬆。同樣最多可領  1.6 億股（搭配  5 年最⾼  600 億美元的採
購承諾），但只看採購量與出貨⾥程碑，沒有設定股價⾨檻。
換句話說，就算  AMD 股價原地踏步，只要  Meta 買夠多晶⽚，股票⼀樣拿得
到。
⽤⽬前的股數換算，兩家巨頭最多各⾃可以拿到 AMD ⼤約⼀成的股份（若 3.2
億股全數發出，現有股東的持股⽐例將被稀釋約 16.0% ）。
這筆交易的本質，就是拿原有股東的股權，去補貼⼤客戶轉換平台的建置成
本。
3.2 財報上的反直覺怪象：五成⽑利背後的「折價券」與業外損
失
你可能會好奇，為什麼第三季 AMD 官⽅財報（ GAAP ）的每股盈餘只有 1.46 美
元，但調整後（ Non-GAAP ）卻⾼達 2.09 美元，中間差了 0.63 美元？
翻開財報調節表，除了常⾒的員⼯股票薪酬（ 5.23 億美元）與併購攤銷（ 5.34
億美元）以及所得稅調整的抵銷（約負 10.73 億美元，即每股抵銷 0.64 美元）
之外，單⼀最⼤的⼀塊調整項，就來⾃這批承諾送給客戶的認股權證：

### Page 10

當作折價券直接扣營收（拉低⽑利率的隱形推⼿）：
根據會計規則，當  AMD 開始向  OpenAI 與  Meta 出貨時，送出去的權證價值會
按⽐例當作給客戶的「銷售折扣（營收折讓）」，直接從營收裡扣掉。
這正好解開了重點⼀的謎團：為什麼第四季新增資料中⼼營收的邊際⽑利率只
有  50% 左右？
因為裡⾯有⼀部分利潤，其實就是以「送股票當折價券」的形式讓利給了⼤客
戶。
每季重新計價的「⽋條」損失：
在客戶還沒真正把權證換成股票之前，會計規則把這批承諾要給的股票視為
AMD ⽋客戶的「負債（⽋條）」，⽽且每⼀季都要按照最新股價與出貨進度重
新計算這張⽋條值多少錢。
第三季因為  AMD 股價上漲、出貨順利，這張⽋條跟著變貴了，導致  AMD 必
須在官⽅損益表上認列⾼達  10.97 億美元（每股  0.66 美元）的業外帳⾯損失。

### Page 11

這會造成⼀個⾮常反直覺的財報現象：未來只要 AMD 晶⽚賣得越好、股價
漲得越⾼，官⽅ GAAP 損益表上的「帳⾯損失」反⽽會跟著變⼤。
（⾄於實際的股數增加與稀釋，財務⻑表⽰要等 2027 年客戶陸續達成出貨
條件並換成股票時才會發⽣。）
3.3 怎麼判斷客戶是不是衝著送股票來的？把名單分成兩組看
既然送股票的代價這麼⾼，我們該怎麼確認客戶買 AMD 是因為產品真的好
⽤？最實際的做法，是把⼤客戶分成兩組來追蹤：
有附帶條件的客戶： OpenAI 與  Meta （拿到認股權證，且  AMD 近期也宣
布投資  Meta 10 億美元），以及  Anthropic （合約中包含  AMD 反過來投資
Anthropic 的承諾）。
純商業採購的客戶（無附帶條件）： Microsoft 、 Oracle ，以及這次新宣
布、透過  HPE 出貨給雲端服務商  Vultr 的  12 億美元  Helios 機櫃⼤單。

### Page 12

追蹤 Microsoft 、 Oracle 與 Vultr 這類「不拿免費股票也願意掏錢」的客戶訂單⽐
重，會是接下來檢驗 AMD 真實產品定價權最直接的溫度計。
重點四｜同樣遇到 PC 賣不動，為什麼
AMD 沒像 Intel ⼀樣暴雷？
4.1 伺服器 CPU 都在⼤賺，差別在於「包袱有多重」
10 ⽉底 Intel 發出財報警訊時，市場⼀度擔⼼ AMD 也會跟著遭殃。
但其實翻開 Intel 財報， Intel 第三季伺服器 CPU 出貨量年增了 10% ，平均售價更
⼤漲 54% ，帶動資料中⼼營收⼤增 78% 來到 73 億美元。
Intel 真正的痛點有兩個：

### Page 13

第⼀， PC 業務仍佔它總營收將近⼀半（ 81 億美元），當 PC 買氣下滑，⽴刻把第
四季總營收指引拖累到 155 億美元；
第⼆， Intel ⾃⼰養了昂貴的晶圓廠，不管⼯廠有沒有訂單，每季都得付龐⼤的
機器折舊與維護費，⼀旦 PC 銷量變少，產能利⽤率下滑的⼯廠就會嚴重吃掉
利潤。
4.2 AMD 的兩⼤優勢：逾六成營收靠資料中⼼、晶圓產能靈活
調度
AMD 同樣沒躲過記憶體漲價與 PC 買氣降溫。
第三季 PC 與電競部⾨營收年減 8% 來到 37 億美元，第四季更預計衰退超過
20% ；管理層甚⾄在法說會上坦⾔，因為記憶體與零組件漲價壓抑終端買氣，
內部在規劃 2027 年財務⽬標時，已經直接假設「 2027 年全球 PC 出貨量將出現
雙位數百分⽐（ 10% 以上）的衰退」。

### Page 14

但即便把這麼悲觀的 PC 假設放進模型裡， AMD 卻依然能交出明年總營收成⻑
破 80% 的超預期指引，關鍵就在於兩⼤體質差異：
資料中⼼已經⻑成絕對主⼒：
AMD 的資料中⼼營收佔⽐已從去年的  47% 拉升到⽬前的  64% （第四季將逼近
75% ，明年更將超過  80% ），靠著伺服器  CPU 與  AI 晶⽚雙引擎，輕鬆填補了
PC 的缺⼝。
把台積電晶圓轉去做⾼⽑利產品：
雖然  AMD 對台積電也有產能採購承諾，但因為台積電的⾼階晶圓產能⾮常吃
緊，當  PC 需求變弱時，蘇姿丰在法說會上指出， AMD 剛好可以把原本要做
PC 的晶圓與載板封裝產能，靈活調度去做⽑利更⾼、供不應求的資料中⼼產
品。
Intel 則剛好相反， PC ⼀弱，⾃家⼯廠的產能利⽤率就跟著掉。

### Page 15

同樣遇到  PC 逆⾵， AMD 是在升級產品組合， Intel 則是被迫吸收龐⼤的⼯
廠固定成本。
重點五｜搶產能與拼軟體：記憶體漲價
下， ROCm 10 扮演什麼⾓⾊？
5.1 記憶體⼤廠開始漲價，成本誰來吸收？
前⾯提到，決定 AMD 2027 年能否衝上 940 億美元的關鍵在於供應鏈產能。
每⼀顆 AI 晶⽚裡都必須搭配⾼頻寬記憶體（ HBM ），⽽記憶體⼤廠 SK hynix 已
經宣布與 14 家⼤客戶簽下⻑約，把 2027 到 2028 年過半的產能都預售光了，還
加⼊了隨市場⾏情調漲價格的條款。
當上游產能被搶光，記憶體廠⾃然掌握了漲價話語權。
蘇姿丰在法說會上坦承零組件確實⾯臨漲價壓⼒，⽬前正與客戶協商「共同分
攤成本」。
這筆多出來的硬體成本，最終有多少能轉嫁給雲端巨頭、有多少得由 AMD ⾃
⼰吸收，將直接影響明年的⽑利率表現。
5.2 為什麼軟體是破局關鍵？幫客戶省下「⼯程師的加班費」
在硬體成本變貴的背景下， AMD 推出最新 ROCm 10 軟體系統的重要性就更加

凸顯了。
我們站在雲端客戶的⾓度想⼀想：
買晶⽚硬體只是第⼀步。如果你買 AMD 的晶⽚省了 20% 硬體費，卻得派幾百
名⾼薪⼯程師花三個⽉改寫程式、解決當機問題才能讓機房順利運轉，那浪費
掉的時間與⼯程師薪⽔，早就把省下的硬體錢吃光了。
ROCm 10 主打讓⼤型機櫃買回去就能快速啟動、⾃動調校效能，⽬的就是把
客戶的「使⽤⾨檻與⿇煩程度」降到最低。
這也是為什麼 AMD 在第三季同步出⼿完成了兩筆關鍵收購：
⼀是收購由 AI 視覺教⺟李⾶⾶創⽴的空間智慧新創 World Labs ，並延攬李⾶⾶
直接出任 AMD ⾸席科學家，從最前沿的模型研發端開始與硬體及 ROCm 軟體
深度綁定；

### Page 16

⼆是收購客製化極速推論晶⽚新創 Taalas ，強化超低延遲的推論能⼒。
只要軟體與模型⽣態系越好⽤、幫客戶省下的⼯程師時間與維護成本越
多， AMD 就越不需要靠硬體殺價或送股票來搶⽣意。
重點六｜帳⾯⼤賺 35 億，為什麼進⼝
袋的現⾦卻少掉六成？
6.1 拆解 33 億美元的「做⽣意墊款」
第三季 AMD 在調整後的損益表上⼤賺了 34.7 億美元，但真正流進公司⼝袋、
可以⾃由動⽤的現⾦（⾃由現⾦流）卻只有 5.54 億美元。
無論是跟上⼀季的 15.58 億美元、還是去年同期的 15.30 億美元相⽐，都⼤幅縮
⽔了 64% 。

### Page 17

第三季買設備與蓋廠房的資本⽀出為 6.48 億美元（⽐第⼆季的 8.08 億美元還
少），真正把現⾦吸⾛的，是⾼達 33 億美元的「⽇常做⽣意墊款」（營運資⾦淨
流出）。
要做近千億美元的⼤⽣意，在收到客戶貨款前， AMD 得先在三個地⽅⾃掏腰包
墊錢：
買原料囤貨（多壓了近  30 億美元）：
倉庫裡的存貨總額攀升到  115 億美元（平均要放  169 天才能賣出，⽐上季的
145 天更久），主要是為了第四季與明年的  MI455 晶⽚及  Helios 系統提前備
料。
付訂⾦鎖產能（多付了約  10 億美元）：
預付款項增加了  10 億美元。對照前⾯  SK hynix 要求簽⻑約鎖產能，這筆錢很
⼤⼀部分就是為了幫  2027 年搶下記憶體與封裝產能所付出的現⾦訂⾦。
客戶拿了貨還沒付錢（應收帳款多了約  19 億美元）：
貨已經出給客戶並算進營收了，但客戶平均要  63 天後才會把錢匯過來（⽐上
季的  57 天拉⻑）。
值得注意的是，現在買  AI 晶⽚的除了  Microsoft 、 Meta 這種現⾦滿⼿的巨頭

外，也包含⼀部分還在⼤量燒錢的  AI 新創實驗室，未來這筆帳款能不能準時
收回，值得持續追蹤。

### Page 18

把上⾯三項加起來，流出⾦額⾼達約 59 億美元；扣掉 AMD ⾃⼰延後付給供應
商的貨款（應付帳款與其他負債增加約 26 億美元）之後，算下來的營運資⾦淨
流出就是 33 億美元。
6.2 公司不缺錢，但算估值時要把「墊款成本」算進去
財務⻑已經預告，為了⽀撐明年的⾼成⻑， 2027 年還是得繼續掏現⾦買庫存、
鎖產能。
不過⼤家不⽤擔⼼ AMD 會資⾦周轉不靈。
截⾄第三季末， AMD 帳上躺著⾼達 158 億美元的現⾦與短期投資，⽽借款（總
債務）只有 71 億美元（其中包含今年 8 ⽉為了提前儲備營運糧草所發⾏的 47.5
億美元公司債），財務家底⾮常厚實。

### Page 19

損益表上的 EPS 告訴你東⻄賣掉後帳⾯賺多少；⽽⾃由現⾦流則誠實地提醒
我們，為了撐起這場千億美元的營收爆發， AMD 必須先掏出多少現⾦去墊
款備料。
重點七｜從⼤跌 25% 到盤後反彈：現
在約 510 美元的 AMD 到底貴不貴？
7.1 現在的股價到底在反映什麼？
財報公布前，外資對 AMD 2027 年 EPS 的預估平均約 15.7 美元。以這個數字計
算， 10 ⽉初 649.42 美元的股價約是 41.4 倍本益⽐，財報前跌到 483.90 美元時約
是 30.8 倍。

### Page 20

現在管理層喊出 2027 年營收成⻑超過 80% ，我們重算⼀次：
營收與⽑利： 營收抓  940 億美元，⽑利率約  54% ，總⽑利約  508 億美
元。
營業費⽤給兩種假設： 營收成⻑  80% ，費⽤不必跟著漲這麼多，但  AMD
每年都要推新⼀代晶⽚、加碼軟體投資，費⽤也不會原地不動。樂觀⼀點
抓  175 億美元（成⻑約  25% ），保守⼀點抓  190 億美元（成⻑約  35% ）。
淨利： 扣掉  13% 稅率，全年淨利約落在  276 億到  289 億美元。
每股盈餘： 如果把明年部分解鎖的客戶權證算進去（假設股數增加到約
17.6 億股）， EPS ⼤約是  15.7 到  16.4 美元。
有趣的是，費⽤抓 190 億美元時算出的 15.7 美元，幾乎和財報前的市場共識⼀
模⼀樣。 Bernstein 先前預估營收 938 億美元， EPS 也只有 15.82 美元。
營收⽬標往上調了⼀⼤截，每股盈餘卻沒跟著動多少。多出來的⽣意，被五成
左右的邊際⽑利、送出去的股票和增加的費⽤分掉了。

### Page 21

拿 15.7 到 16.4 美元對照盤後約 510 美元的股價，遠期本益⽐⼤約是 31 到 32.5
倍。
本益⽐之外，還有⼀個數字要⼀起看。
現在 AMD 市值約 8,540 億美元，如果 2027 年淨利 280 億美元能有⼋成變成⾃由
現⾦流，股價約是⾃由現⾦流的 38 倍；如果只有五成，就變成 61 倍。第三季
這個⽐例只有 16% 。
所以現在的 510 美元，⼤致是「相信 940 億美元營收會達標，也相信明年的
現⾦會跟著回來」。
營收和現⾦這兩件事少了任何⼀件，現價都不算便宜。
7.2 給讀者的三個估值參考座標

### Page 22

AMD 現在訂單⽐產能多，所以明年真正的變數是能拿到多少記憶體與封裝產
能、⽤什麼成本出貨。
以下三個情境都⽤上⾯同⼀套公式計算，再搭配不同的本益⽐。（註：此為測
試獲利與本益⽐變化的算術情境，並⾮⽬標價預測。）
保守情境（產能卡關、記憶體漲價吃掉⽑利）： 營收  880 億美元、⽑利率
53.5% 、費⽤  185 億美元， EPS 約  14.3 美元。客戶買得少，解鎖的權證也
少，稀釋較輕。乘以  25 倍，約  357 美元。
基準情境（產能⼤致到位、達標  940 億美元）： EPS 約  15.7 到  16.4 美
元，乘以  31 倍，約  487 到  510 美元，⼤致就是⽬前的盤後股價。
樂觀情境（拿到更多產能、成本成功轉嫁給客戶）： 營收  1,000 億美元、
⽑利率  56% 、費⽤  180 億美元， EPS 約  18.6 美元。這時客戶買得多，解
鎖的權證也多，股數假設約  17.8 億股。乘以  35 倍，約  650 美元，剛好回
到  10 ⽉初的⾼點。

樂觀情境裡有個⼩諷刺： AMD 賣得越好，送出去的股票就越多。
結論
回頭看財報前那 25% （約 165 美元）的跌幅，我們可以⽤⽂章裡的數字拆成兩
塊來算。
第⼀塊是 PC 恐慌。
事實上，管理層在給出「明年總營收成⻑破 80% 」的⽬標時，內部早就已經把
「 2027 年全球 PC 出貨量衰退雙位數百分⽐」的悲觀預期算進去了。
就算我們假設 2027 年 PC 與電競部⾨再多衰退兩成，少掉的營收⼤約是 24 到 28
億美元（以第四季單季約 30 億美元推算），在 50% ⽑利率與 13% 稅率假設下，
換算成 EPS ⼤約只影響 0.7 美元。
⽤ 30 倍本益⽐計算，⼤概只值 20 美元出頭的股價；就算⽤財報前 41 倍的本益
⽐來算，也不到 30 美元。
Intel 暴雷那天 AMD 單⽇跌掉近 11% ，跌幅遠遠超過 PC 業務真正能造成的傷
害。
第⼆塊是本益⽐從 41 倍回到 30 倍左右，這⼀段反⽽有它的道理。

### Page 23

要讓  649 美元的股價在 30 倍本益⽐下站得住腳， AMD 2027 年的 EPS 得做到
21.6 美元左右；⽤同樣的營收與費⽤往回推，全公司⽑利率必須拉⾼到 63% 上
下。
但這份財報透露的⽅向剛好相反：新增資料中⼼營收的邊際⽑利率⼤約五成、
送給 OpenAI 與 Meta 的權證會稀釋股本並折減營收，搶產能⼜持續壓著現⾦
流。
營收的⾼成⻑已經被證實了，但賺錢的品質還需要時間證明。
⽽這被殺的估值倍數未來能不能再賺回來，就要看接下來這  4 個核⼼指
標：
1. Q4 資料中⼼爆發⼒： 第四季資料中⼼營收能否如期單季⼤增  50% （多賣
約  43 億美元）？
2. ⽑利率防線： 在邊際⽑利約  50% 的新訂單放量、權證折扣攤提，以及記
憶體漲價的分攤協商下，全公司⽑利率能否穩守  55% ？
3. 純商業訂單的拓展： Microsoft 、 Oracle 與  Vultr 這類「不拿免費股票」的
標準訂單，佔⽐能否持續擴⼤？
4. 現⾦回收的速度： 壓在倉庫裡的  115 億美元存貨與  19 億美元新增應收帳

款，明年能否順利變成現⾦流回⼝袋？
AMD 已經向市場證明了⾃⼰搶下近千億營收規模的實⼒；⽽在下⼀個階段，股
價能不能重回前⾼，將取決於這場盛宴過後，最終有多少利潤能真正轉化為⾃
由現⾦流，留進股東的⼝袋裡。

## Document 374: LatAm’s silicon dividend_ tailwinds from the AI investment boom in the U.S. (2026-10-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/LatAm’s silicon dividend_ tailwinds from the AI  investment boom in the U.S. (2026-10-28).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-10-28
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

LatAm’s silicon dividend: 
tailwinds from the AI  
investment boom in the U.S.

1 
Authors 
Fancisco Campos 
Chief LatAm Economist

Beatriz Garcia Nunes 
LatAm Economist

October 28, 2026 
Key messages: 
- U.S. investment has broken away from Europe and China since 2023, 
powered by AI-related equipment, software and research spending. 
- The AI CapEx boom demands commodities and merchandise that LatAm 
can supply.  
- Latin America’s export gains are real but concentrated. Copper, computer 
and electronics products provide the clearest links to the U.S. AI buildout. 
- Chile is the region’s most direct copper beneficiary. Mexico captures the 
windfall stemming from increases hardware demand and trade-diversion. In 
these cases, the AI-related tailwinds have undergirded appreciable 
improvements in external accounts. 
- The cases for nickel and zinc illustrate that the dividend is not widespread. 
U.S. imports of the former have mostly returned toward pre-pandemic levels, 
while for the latter bilateral gains partly reflect supplier substitution. 
- While expanding AI use, supply shortages and incentives can extend the 
CapEx boom, its financial viability poses downside risks. 
Another ‘American exceptionalism’ story  
Investment has become one of the sharpest dividing lines among the world’s 
core economic blocs. Since 2024, U.S. gross fixed capital formation has firmed 
up appreciably. Meanwhile, investment in Europe has broadly stalled while in 
China’s it has retreated, the drivers of which are beyond the scope of this report 
(Figure 1).  
The U.S. divergence has largely been an AI story: a burst of spending on 
computing power, digital infrastructure and intellectual property has offset 
weakness in conventional construction and housing. Indeed, total U.S. fixed 
investment is nearly 12% above its 2023 level. Within it, investment in 
information-processing equipment has risen by roughly 57%, software outlays by 
about 35%, and research and development by around 15%. By contrast, non-
residential structures and residential investment sit below their 2023 levels 
(Figure 2).

### Page 2

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 2

Figure 1: The global investment divergence

Figure 2: AI-related categories lead U.S. investment

Source: US Bureau of Economic Analysis, Eurostat, National Bureau of Statistics of 
China, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Source: US Bureau of Economic Analysis, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Latin America’s commodity channel 
AI is not merely a semiconductor story. Chips require specialized minerals; data 
centers require electricity and cooling; electricity requires grids, transformers and 
storage. That chain pulls in copper, aluminum, silver, tin, lithium and selected 
semiconductor inputs, along with natural gas and uranium. Latin America has 
meaningful exposure to several of these inputs, but copper has emerged as the 
commodity with the cleanest and most economically significant regional link. 
Copper is used throughout the AI infrastructure stack, including power cables, 
substations, transformers, server boards, heat sinks and cooling systems. Rising 
U.S. copper demand has lifted imports from Chile and Peru, particularly the 
former (Figure 3). T he effect matters beyond merchandise export volumes. 
Favorable terms-of-trade have helped Chile generate a current -account surplus 
for the first time in roughly fifteen years (0.2% of GDP as of Q2 2026 on a 4 -
quarter rolling basis ) and have allowed Peru to log the largest surplus in about 
forty-five years (4.5% of GDP as of Q2 2026 on a 4-quarter rolling basis).

28 October 2026

### Page 3

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 3

Figure 3: Copper epitomized the commodity link

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Not every metal is copper 
The broader mining story demands more caution, though. U.S. nickel imports have 
recovered toward pre-Covid levels, but the upswing has not displayed the same 
persistence as copper. Mexico has captured a disproportionate share of the 
bilateral improvement, yet that gain looks more like normalization than a 
sustained AI -driven break (Figure 4). Zinc tells a similar story. U.S. real imports 
stand shy of their pre-pandemic levels, and stronger purchases from Mexico and 
Peru partly reflect a reallocation away from Brazil rather than a decisive increase 
in U.S. demand (Figure 5).

Figure 4: Nickel: recovery, not a boom

Figure 5: Zinc: a substitution story

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

28 October 2026

### Page 4

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 4

Mexico’s hardware advantage 
The larger non-commodity prize is in computers and electronics. U.S. imports of 
these products have sky -rocketed, while the supplier mix has shifted away from 
China. Mexico and various Asian economies have gained share on the back of this 
double-whammy impulse (Figure 6). 
This helps explain why Mexico’s non-automotive manufacturing exports, notably 
electronics, have outpaced automotive shipments to the U.S. and pressures from 
higher oil prices. The resulting non -oil trade surplus has offset pressure from the 
oil balance and helped Mexico generate a balanced current account (0% of GDP 
as of Q2 2026 on a 4 -quarter rolling basis). The external boost is meaningful, 
although it sho uld not necessarily be interpreted as bona fide  evidence of 
nearshoring, in our view.

Figure 6: U.S. computer & electronics imports going into overdrive

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research.

How much runway remains? 
Quantifying or mapping out a timeframe for the AI investment outlook is beyond 
the scope of this piece or our expertise. Nevertheless, we’d venture to argue that 
the capex cycle appears more likely to mature gradually than to stop abruptly . 
One reason is that the investment case is shifting from frontier -model training 
toward enterprise inference . Capacity that once served development can 
increasingly run commercial workloads for companies. That creates a path to 
revenue even if artificial general intelligence (AGI) crystallizes later than 
expected. It also spreads demand to a much larger corporate customer base.  
A second reason is scarcity. Graphics processing units (GPUs) remain tight, while 
power availability, grid connections, cooling equipment, construction capacity 
and data -center complexity limit how quickly supply can respond. Bottlenecks 
may therefore exte nd the cycle. The constraint is especially relevant for copper 
28 October 2026

### Page 5

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 5

and power infrastructure, where projects have long lead times and permitting 
hurdles. 
A third reason is strategic. AI development resembles a prisoner’s dilemma at 
both the corporate and national levels. Each participant may worry about costs, 
safety and control, yet slowing unilaterally risks surrendering technological, 
commercial or natio nal security advantages. The same tension is visible in calls 
for guardrails alongside continued spending. Competition can therefore sustain 
capex even when short-term returns are uncertain. 
We acknowledge the risks, nonetheless. Higher borrowing costs raise the hurdle 
rate for an infrastructure buildout financed partly through debt. More important, 
revenues must eventually justify the installed capital base. The bearish case is not 
that AI la cks value; it is that infrastructure spending may run ahead of 
monetization, compromising some companies’ repayment capacity. Public 
opposition to the development of AI infrastructure and/or adoption of AI 
technologies could also slow down the technology’s roll-out. 
The regional balance  
Summing up, the AI investment boom in the U .S. is delivering a dividend to parts 
of Latin America, not the region as a whole. Chile and Peru benefit through copper 
prices and export volumes. Mexico benefits through electronics manufacturing 
and the replacement of Chinese supply. For Brazil and Colombia, the AI dividend 
is rather muted. Where materialized, furthermore, the dividend supports trade 
and current accounts more clearly rather than improving the domestic 
investment. 
These dynamics should persist while U.S. AI investment remains supply -
constrained and strategically protected. If widespread adoption advances and 
the strategic race intensifies, the impulse can last. If financing costs rise further 
or monetization disappo ints, the cycle may narrow. Either way, copper and 
Mexican electronics remain the region’s most visible gauges of the U.S. AI 
investment appetite.

28 October 2026

TODAY’S INSIGHTS
Financial Markets
Markets were generally buoyed overnight, with Anthropic’s
IPO filing and Meta’s US$25bn equity offering closing five
times oversubscribed adding momentum back into the
AI trade, while softer oil prices provided further support.

Attention now turns to a series of key economic developments,
with Australian CPI due today alongside policy decisions and
accompanying guidance from the RBNZ, BoC and FOMC.
• US equities moved higher, supported by AI-related
stocks. The S&P 500 gained 0.8%, while the NASDAQ
outperformed, rising 1.2%, and the Dow Jones was
unchanged. European markets were mixed, with the Euro
Stoxx 50 up 0.8%, while the FTSE 100 fell 0.8% ahead of
the UK Budget.
• Tech-heavy Asian markets were buoyed as recent headlines

helped ease concerns around AI financing. The KOSPI led
gains, rising 3.1%, while the Nikkei 225 also performed
strongly, up 1.7%. The Hang Seng diverged from regional
peers, falling 0.7%. Locally, the ASX 200 gained 0.5%.
• US Treasury yields were little changed, with the 2-year
yield rising 1bp and the 10-year yield unchanged. Futures
markets remain cautious about pricing further Fed tightening
in 2026, with the probability of a hike at today’s FOMC

meeting sitting at just 7%, taking the implied year-end fed
funds rate to 4.10%.
• Australian government bond yields were little changed
ahead of today’s CPI release, with the 3-year yield rising
1bp and the 10-year yield falling 1bp. Swap markets remain
cautious around additional RBA tightening in 2026, with the
probability of a November rate hike priced at 62%, taking
the implied year-end cash rate to 4.80%.
• Pre-FOMC positioning supported the US dollar, with

the DXY up 0.3%. The AUD outperformed other major
currencies, rising 0.2% against the greenback to 0.7028 and
also gaining across the major crosses. The euro and yen both
fell 0.3% against the USD, while the sterling declined 0.2%.
• Oil prices declined on hopes that diplomacy could avert a
renewed escalation in the US-Iran conflict. Brent fell 1.6%
to US$114/bbl, while WTI declined 1.9% to US$99.9/bbl.
Dated Brent traded at US$121/bbl. Gold rose 1.1% ahead

of today’s FOMC meeting, while positive risk sentiment
supported base metals, with copper up 1.0% and aluminium
gaining 1.5%. Iron ore fell 1.1%, remaining below US$90/t
amid persistent concerns around global demand.
International Data
US durable goods orders rose 1.8% in September after a 0.1%
gain in August. Growth for core orders (non defence, ex aircraft)
was more modest in September at 0.5% but follows a 1.6% rise
in August.
US S&P Cotality Case Shiller house prices rose a further 0.3%

in August, accelerating annual growth at the margin to 2.7%yr.
US new home sales fell 0.6% in September following a
downwardly revised 5.1% gain (prev 6.4%) in August. Sales are
currently near the average of the past 5 years.
US Conference Board consumer confidence jumped from 84.2
to 91.7 in October on an improvement in current conditions
and expectations. While still below average, this measure of
consumer confidence continues to outperform the University of

Michigan survey, held up by the robust strength of the labour
market. Responses to the Michigan measure instead look to be
focused more on cost of living.
The Richmond Fed manufacturing index was unchanged at -2
in October.
Local Data
Our New Zealand team published their 2026 General Election
preview, outlining the major parties’ key policies (see here).

关闭上海淮海路世界旗舰店七个⽉后， MUJI 無印良品找到了新的落脚点。  11 ⽉ 6 ⽇，第九
届中国国际进⼝ ...
摘要：
关闭上海淮海路世界旗舰店七个⽉后， MUJI 無印良品找到了新的落脚点。
11 ⽉ 6 ⽇，第九届中国国际进⼝博览会期间， MUJI 与淮海集团完成签约，宣布全新上海世界旗舰
店将落⼾淮海中路 887 号。
新店计划集合零售、餐饮及住宿等多种业态。 MUJI ⽅⾯向华尔街⻅闻介绍，项⽬预计拥有上下
六层、超过 8000 平⽅⽶的空间，并将引⼊上海⾸家 MUJI HOTEL 。酒店规模预计超过北京、深
圳现有项⽬，成为品牌在国内规模最⼤的酒店。
⽬前，新店的具体开业时间尚未确定，最快有望于 2027 年开业。
值得关注的是，这次新址落定，恰好与优⾐库在淮海路的旗舰店搬迁形成了⼀次交换。
今年 3 ⽉ 31 ⽇， MUJI 位于淮海中路 755 号的世界旗舰店正式闭店。这家开业于 2015 年 12 ⽉、经
营⾯积约 3438 平⽅⽶的⻔店，曾集合零售、餐饮等⽣活⽅式业态，是 MUJI 在中国的重要标志性

⻔店。
彼时， MUJI ⽅⾯解释称，闭店主要由于租约到期，⻔店经营业绩仍位居全国前列，并表⽰正在
推进新的世界旗舰店选址。
7 个⽉后，优⾐库位于 755 号的全新全球旗舰店正式开业，接⼿了 MUJI 此前的经营空间。新店销
售⾯积约 2800 平⽅⽶，覆盖三层空间。
⽽优⾐库此前的全球旗舰店，恰恰位于淮海中路 887 号，也就是 MUJI 此次宣布签约的新址。
两家⽇本零售品牌由此在淮海路完成了⼀次颇为特殊的旗舰店址互换。
优⾐库此前在 887 号经营的是集合 GU 、 Comptoir des Cotonniers 等迅销集团旗下品牌的综合旗
舰店；如今迁⼊约 2800 平⽅⽶的新店，仅保留优⾐库品牌，更加聚焦服装零售与个性化服务。
MUJI 则计划在 887 号打造超过 8000 平⽅⽶的综合空间，将零售延伸⾄餐饮、住宿等业态。
新旗舰店的落地，也发⽣在 MUJI 中国业务恢复增⻓的阶段。
截⾄ 2026 年 8 ⽉底的财年内， MUJI ⺟公司良品计画营业收⼊达到 9235.92 亿⽇元，同⽐增⻓

17.7% ；营业利润 1034.36 亿⽇元，同⽐增⻓ 40.1% ，均创历史新⾼。
其中，中国⼤陆市场实现收⼊和利润双增⻓，既有⻔店及电商业务销售额同⽐增⻓ 16.3% 。财年
内， MUJI 在中国⼤陆新开 42 家⻔店，期末⻔店数量达到 433 家。
中国⼤陆仍是 MUJI 最⼤的海外市场。伴随业绩恢复，品牌也在继续扩⼤⻔店⽹络，推进既有⻔
店改装升级。

## Document 398: Open mouth operations (2026-11-07)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Open mouth operations (2026-11-07).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#australia #luciellis #rba
Open mouth operations
By Luci Ellis
Chief Economist Westpac Group, Westpac
13:34 October 23 2026 Share
The RBA does not want to promise what it will do, but it is increasingly trying to tell us what
it thinks.
Central banks’ communication is carefully scrutinised for every nuance. Lately, there seems to have
been more RBA communication to pore over. The method of delivery has also evolved. This has

### Page 2

Market participants pore over every utterance by central banks for clues to future rate moves. They will
have plenty to pore over in the run-up to the November RBA decision. After the flurry of fireside chats
this week, ’60 Minutes’ will run a pre-recorded interview with chief economist Sarah Hunter on Sunday.
Next Wednesday, the whole senior policy team will appear before Senate Estimates, just as the
quarterly CPI is released. (This timing will have been the Senate’s choice, not the RBA’s.) After the

decision will come the Statement on Monetary Policy, a media release and press conference. More
speeches will follow, including one by the Deputy Governor on the Thursday.
RBA communication does seem to have changed, not just following the RBA Review but also more
recently. There may be fewer policy meetings each year, but the communication outside that certainly
feels more frequent than a couple of years ago. Back in 2024, speaking engagements felt a little

sparse outside the post-meeting media conference and parliamentary appearances. We have counted
58 public engagements so far this year on the Speeches page on the RBA’s website. This compares
with 58 for the whole of last year and 52 in 2024. Excluding the ‘compulsory’ press conferences and
parliamentary appearances, fewer than half of the 2024 events were related to economics, markets
and monetary policy.
The ‘how’ has changed
The way the RBA communicates with markets has changed as well. There are fewer set-piece

speeches on policy and a lot more semi-scripted ‘fireside chats’. Compared with a few years ago, there
are more interviews across a wide range of media. This is a big change from the pre-Review world. It
was a huge deal when the previous Governor appeared on the 7:30 Report; it is hard to imagine him
agreeing to an Assistant Governor appearing on 60 Minutes.
Part of the change reflects increased efforts to reach a general audience. Central banks want to explain

why they do what they do. This matters especially when interest rates are rising but the household
sector is not the source of the inflation.
Speaking differently to different audiences can sometimes confuse markets. As the RBA’s Chief
Communication Officer highlighted in a recent panel discussion, the post-meeting media conference is
designed to speak to the public. Market participants and economists certainly tune in, but we are not
sometimes caused confusion when tone seems to vary. Different audiences call for different tone and

varying complexity.
The content has evolved, too. RBA officials are increasingly using speeches directed to professional
audiences to discuss more technical estimates of inferred variables such as neutral rates and
financial conditions. They are also highlighting that monetary policy combats inflation in more ways
than just squeezing mortgage holders.
One thing has not changed: the RBA is not in the business of providing ‘forward guidance’ promises

about the future path of interest rates. It does, however, give more running commentary about how it
is interpreting recent data. These interpretations are themselves useful signals about what the RBA
thinks it might do.

### Page 3

the primary audience. So if you think that what the Governor said in the media conference sounded
more ‘dovish’ than what another official says in a more market-oriented forum, it is partly because she
is trying to convey that the RBA understands that its recent policy actions are making things harder for
many Australians. But this is in much the same way that a doctor might say, “This might hurt a bit.” It
does, but that will not stop the treatment.

A related issue is that it is easier to control tone in pre-drafted material such as the minutes and set-
piece speeches than in unstructured Q&A. Senior central bankers are trained to be careful with their
words in unscripted settings. If the tone differs, the minutes are still more likely to say what the Board
really means than an off-the-cuff answer.
Greater communication with the broader public might have reduced the pressure to make the set-piece

speeches more accessible. Research on central bank communication often uses measures such as
the Flesch–Kincaid Grade Level and Flesch Reading Ease score. A comprehensive analysis was
beyond the scope of this note. However, a spot comparison suggests four speeches from 2026 (1, 2, 3,
4) were harder to read than four from 2021 (1, 2, 3, 4). The 2021 speeches scored in the 11 to 12
range, which is high-school-level complexity. The 2026 speeches scored between 12 and 15, which is

academic or technically dense text requiring university-level education. (Including two of my own 2021
speeches is an obvious caveat, but the two speeches by then-Governor Phil Lowe also scored better
on these metrics than the sample from this year. It should be noted, though, that readability metrics do
not necessarily imply better clarity if the concepts discussed are more abstract.)
The ‘what’ has changed
As noted above, the content of RBA communication has evolved, too. As its intellectual framework has

begun to put more weight on the balance of supply and demand, and those invisible ‘star’ variables
(NAIRUs, neutral real rates, ‘financial conditions’, etc.), the communication to professional audiences
has increasingly focused on those topics.
More recently, the RBA has also placed more emphasis on the full range of transmission channels from
interest rates to inflation (though not, as we have noted previously, the labour supply channel). This is

understandable in a world of supply shocks. People need to understand that monetary policy is not
simply an exercise in hitting mortgage borrowers. We doubt they would be comforted, though, by the
Deputy Governor’s explanation this week that the ‘cashflow channel’ was the smallest of the
transmission channels from interest rates to inflation, with the exchange rate much more prominent.
That implies a lot of pain for a small group, for little effect on inflation. It raises the question of whether

other policy tools or institutional arrangements might get the same effect on inflation through the other
channels without hitting a minority of households disproportionately.
We have also seen a move away from using catchphrases to summarise the position. From ‘narrow
path’ to ‘not ruling anything in or out’ to ‘cautious and predictable’, once entrenched, a phrase can
outlast its usefulness.

### Page 4

Related articles
One thing that has not changed is the way the minutes record the presentations to the Board and the
members’ discussion. The minutes rarely convey much new information beyond the post-meeting
statements. Sometimes, though, the minutes reveal the Board’s reaction to the staff’s analysis. The
August 2024 minutes were a good example: the discussion highlighted just how sensitive the forecasts
were to key judgements by the staff.
The ‘why’ has not changed

Despite these changes, the underlying objectives of the RBA’s communication have not changed. It
wants to explain – and perhaps justify – its decisions. And it wants to describe how it interprets
incoming data, especially inflation, the labour market and the balance of supply and demand.
The RBA, like any central bank, is not really in the business of providing ‘forward guidance’ about
future moves. Circumstances change, so it can never guarantee a particular future path for interest

rates. Some academic literature argues that promising to keep rates low for a long time (or high, for
that matter) independently amplifies the transmission of monetary policy beyond the effect of current-
period interest rates alone, by shifting the short end of the yield curve. Central banks including the RBA
have certainly tried jawboning in this way from time to time. The prevailing view, though, is that the
potential cost of reputational loss if things do not turn out as predicted outweighs the benefits of a

slightly stronger transmission mechanism.
In this context, it is the interpretation of data that provides the signal about future policy directions. This
is why it was noteworthy that the Governor still characterised the labour market as ‘a little tight’ after the
September meeting, even though the Board’s post-meeting communication did not. We have been
noting for some time that the RBA staff have a downbeat view of supply capacity. Together with the

RBA’s oft-stated concerns about inflation, this is why the Deputy Governor’s ‘hawkishness’ did not
surprise us.

## Document 468: What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it_ (2026-10-11)

Source PDF: all report pdf/What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it_ (2026-10-11).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-10-11
Pages: 17
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it?
A closer look at the impact of China’s currency on China’s export growth.
Sean Yong/Reuters
UPDATED
October 11, 2026 12:00 a.m.
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
There is an argument—made in the recent Geneva
Report and by various former IMF officials—that
China’s current export surge is the simple
consequence of the housing downturn and the
resulting weakness in Chinese internal demand.*
The weakness in the RMB in this story is entirely
incidental to the increase in exports, which is, more or
less, a mechanical response to weak internal demand.

Cannot sell excavators at home? Export them.
Built more modern car capacity than your internal
market supports? Export that too…
The fall in the nominal RMB over the course of 2022
and the first half of 2023 in nominal and read terms
was simply a function of domestic macroeconomic
weakness, and a growing rate differential. It wasn’t an
active policy choice by China’s central bank.

### Page 3

3
Now, some might note that China’s currency doesn’t
always move with rate differentials. The RMB didn’t
consistently get pulled up when Chinese rates were
higher than U.S. rates (2002-2020 generally
speaking), and while it did fall when rate differentials
moved against the RMB in 2022 and 2023, it then
stabilized and has even appreciated modestly since
the middle of last year. That appreciation hasn’t been
big enough to correct the deep undervaluation of the

RMB, but it came at a time when the PBOC policy
rate (the reverse repo rate) was well below the U.S.
policy rate.
China doesn’t have an open financial account. As a
result, equilibrium in the foreign exchange market is
thus driven far more by exporter conversion and
current account flows than by rate differentials and
financial flows.** That, together with China’s ongoing
management of the exchange rate and a trade surplus
that can generate significant inflows, means that the

### Page 4

4
value of the RMB is much more of a policy variable
under the independent control of the Chinese
authorities than a simple byproduct of monetary and
fiscal policies.
The modern IMF is reluctant to accept this
conclusion. Its surveillance has generally ignored
China’s currency management (even if that
management is relatively obvious; see the data on FX
settlement) and its recent comprehensive assessment
of China’s economy completely ignored the balance of

payments and exchange rate management.
But it is pretty obvious to the market, even if the
PBOC, rather absurdly, insists that “market forces
play a decisive role in determining the RMB
exchange rate.”
I doubt the PBOC can win this argument. I agree
with Mark Sobel:
“The renminbi is still heavily managed and the
authorities have acted to slow appreciation. The paper
states that the PBoC phased out ‘regular’ foreign
exchange intervention in 2017. But it has many

means at its disposal to achieve the same effect
through extensive capital controls, administrative
guidance, signalling at the fix and deploying state-
owned commercial banks. These means are heavily
used. Thus, the word ‘regular’ must be taken with a
pinch of salt. The comparison to Japan, which

### Page 5

5
intervenes occasionally, is spurious – the yen largely
floats freely in integrated global markets. The same
cannot be said for the renminbi.”
In reality, the PBOC’s daily fix now plays the decisive
role in the currency’s path, and the market is often a
bet on how the PBOC’s management of the yuan will
evolve.
That is clear from the lack of volatility in the yuan’s
rate against the dollar. The yuan’s move down against
the dollar in 2022 was a controlled 10 percent move,

and the appreciation over the last 16 months has been
even more controlled crawl.
And the market is clearly massaged with substantial
help from the state banks. In fact that the pattern of
changes in the monthly FX settlement data looks
exactly like the pattern of intervention of a central
bank managing its currency around a tight band (the

### Page 6

6
state banks in practice buy FX to keep the CNY from
testing the strong side of the band, so there is an
asymmetry in how the CNY is managed)
None of this would matter much if China’s economy
was small. But that obviously isn’t the case. Nor would
this matter much if China’s exports didn’t respond to
changes in the RMB’s real (and nominal) value. But
that is also not the case.
There is abundant evidence that China’s exports do
respond to moves in the currency. Periods of RMB

weakness have consistently been associated with
periods of especially rapid Chinese export growth.

### Page 7

7
Chancellor Merz and President Macron got this
right, even if macroeconomic issues haven’t yet
figured prominently in the EU’s dialogue with
China.*** As they note in their open letter to the
European Commission:
“An appreciation of undervalued currencies towards a value
more in line with fundamentals would help support the
rebalancing of trade accounts with the EU in the short and
medium term … European policy discussions focused on
forging a common understanding of these exchange rate issues

and their interaction with global imbalances would be useful.”
The best evidence that exports responded to changes
in the currency, and not simply to changes in the path
of China’s internal demand, is the acceleration in
China’s export growth after the RMB’s depreciation
over the course of 2022.
This is the killer chart.

### Page 8

8
Now, you might ask: what of the acceleration in
export growth in 2020 and 2021? That was pretty
clearly tied to the global increase in goods demand
that came with the pandemic and work from home
requirements. It was expected to fade when the world
economy normalized. But it didn’t. By 2023 Chinese
export growth again topped global trade growth.
The general expectation that the rise in China’s goods
surplus during Covid would prove to be “transitory”

### Page 9

9
heavily influenced the IMF’s work at the time: see the
IMF’s external sector reports of 2023 and 2024.
I would argue that neglecting the exchange rate was
the key reason the IMF’s kept forecasting, incorrectly,
that China’s current account surplus would fall (See
this blog with Shahin Vallee). The IMF certainly
didn’t warn that China’s property downturn and the
RMB’s real depreciation risked a second China shock
in its 2022, 2023, or 2024 surveillance reports.

Concretely, the weaker RMB appears to have
contributed to a very large outperformance of China’s
exports relative to global trade. See the following
chart
That incidentally also happened after the RMB
followed the dollar down in 2003.
In both periods, there were significant structural
shifts. The WTO accession in 2002, and the success

### Page 10

10
of Chinese industrial policies in the “green” industrial
sectors and autos in 2022. In both cses exports were
turbocharged when structural shifts that should have
resulted in a stronger currency were instead amplified
by currency weakness.
No matter. Paul Krugman has noted that a country
that is converging technologically with its trade
partners should see a trend appreciation in its
currency. China got the technological convergence,
but not the real appreciation—at least not until this

year. And the move since the middle of 2025 has
corrected the depreciation of the RMB that occurred
when the RMB followed the dollar down on
liberation day, but not the structural overvaluation.
Now, there is an argument that exchange rate
adjustment is too hot an issue to make it a priority.
The PBOC isn’t interested it seems.
And some believe that in the interest of comity and
good relations with China, the IMF should mirror
the PBOC’s views. After all a stronger RMB would

slow China’s exports and thus slow China’s economy
at a time when China needs export growth to escape
deflation and generate employment. Or so the
argument goes. Concerns about “external balance“
and the negative global spillovers from China’s rising
goods surplus should be ignored and the Fund’s sole
focus should be on policies that lead to internal

### Page 11

11
balance. That provides one rationale for the Fund’s
ongoing decision to essentially ignore the contentious
issue of the PBOC’s ongoing exchange rate
management.
That argument, however, assumes that China’s
economy is small and thus China’s external surplus
can safely be ignored because it isn’t a drag on the
growth of the IMF’s other members.
That clearly isn’t true.
And it isn’t clear that China is more interested in
having a negotiation over its level of basic pension

benefits or the size of its fiscal deficit (where the
IMF continues to make timid recommendations that
would not move the needle—and certainly wouldn’t
meaningfully reduce China’s external surplus) than a
discussion over its currency practices.****
Back in the world of fixed but adjustable exchange
rates, there was an expectation (not always met to be
sure) that surplus countries would allow their
currencies to strengthen to bring their economies

back into external balance, and that other policy tools
would be used to ensure internal balance.
I would argue that China’s exchange rate (against
both the dollar and the basket) has many similarities
to the old world of fixed but adjustable currencies.
True, it moves on a controlled path when it moves

### Page 12

12
between different levels rather than jumping up and
down. But the PBOC does frequently target a level,
and that targeted level does change over time.
I would further argue that steps like the recent tariff
rate quota that will limit China’s exports of hybrid
cars to Europe to around 350,000 units next year (up
from 2025, but well below the 800,000 units of
2026) aren’t going to address the underlying
imbalance on their own. They can provide a bit of a

buffer for specific sectors.
But that buffer, while necessary, won’t address the
broad-based erosion of manufacturing capacity
outside China. This erosion will persist as long as an
undervalued RMB sends the entire world a signal to
use China as its production base.
The new system of managed trade that is emerging in
autos would need to be progressively expanded to an
ever-increasing set of sectors if protection is used as a
substitute for both domestic reform in China and

exchange rate adjustment.
That is now the direction of travel, but it doesn’t
address the underlying systemic issues.
Incidentally, those who believe the path of internal
demand is critical to rebalancing and that exchange
rate moves are secondary need to explain how an
acceleration in domestic demand would, on its own,

### Page 13

13
lead China’s external accounts to adjust. One
important, stylized fact about China is that domestic
demand growth is no longer feeding over to import
growth (ex-gold, that is).
Bottom line: China, which needs a 1.5 percentage
point contribution from net exports every year, is on a
collision course with its trading partners without a
currency correction that provides the basis for more
balanced trade even in the absence of trade
restrictions.
And there isn’t a realistic path towards balance that

doesn’t involve a stronger RMB; there aren’t, as Paul
Krugman once put it, immaculate transfers.
* That downturn shows up in domestic deflationary
pressure, and weak job growth/high rates of (at least
youth) unemployment. China, in other words, isn’t in
“internal balance.” I really liked the report from

### Page 14

14
Kamakshya Trivedi and Hui Shan of Goldman Sachs
arguing China needs two tools to hit two targets
(internal and external balance).
** To be sure, rate differentials matter for exporter
conversion—but so does the expected movement of
the RMB. The market understands this better than
the Fund. The Chen and Suwanapruti (Goldman
Sachs) note uses a framework similar to mine to
describe the CNY’s moves in Q3:
“In our view, sizeable FX inflows have continued to support

RMB appreciation despite unfavorable interest rate
differentials, as strong export receipts and increased exporter
FX conversions have outweighed portfolio outflows. Our
estimated FX conversion ratio rose to 63% in August,
compared with around 45% in normal years, likely reflecting a
shift in market expectations from RMB depreciation to
appreciation.”
*** Š ef č ovi č  has jurisdiction over trade policy
(narrowly defined), but not broader macroeconomic

policies. Europe’s institutional architecture for
exchange rate coordination is not well-developed.
**** The current account surplus is no longer masked
to the same extent as in 2023 and 2024, as SAFE has
fiddled with its BoP methodology once again (per the
Federal Reserve) and the BoP goods surplus is no
longer significantly below the customs goods surplus.
Of course, there are still problems in the data—the
deficit in the investment income account most

事受損，估計全球煉油廠開工率年減近 800萬桶/日，
加上中、俄禁止柴油出口 ，使柴油供應比原油更緊
張。美國柴油庫存低於五年區間下緣 ，柴油價格創
高，因美未禁止柴油出口 ，柴油利潤保持強勁 。但
美協調G7國家釋出柴油儲備為期四個月 ，達成美俄

柴油供應協議增加供給 ，雖粗略換算為短期每日供
給增加不到全球 1%，預期對柴油價格壓力影響僅
5%內，明年隨設備漸修復、地緣風險降低後，才緩
降，但美國超額柴油利潤恐見頂。
《4Q26能源類股較看好綜合油企》
預估美綜合油企 4Q26受惠油價高於預期 、LNG出
口增加，柴油高裂解價差 ，但進入汽油需求淡季 ，
獲利季比下滑 ，但年比仍大幅成長 ，現金流仍強大 ，
維持資本紀律及股東回報 。雖成品油供應仍較原油
吃緊，但煉油商超額利潤恐見頂 ，較看好綜合油企 。
